The increased demands disrupted her usual balance of solitary painting, gardening, and quiet walks, and she began spending more nights awake planning projects and studying, insisting she could manage on very little rest. Over time, coworkers noticed her documentation became inconsistent and she grew intensely suspicious about others trying to undermine her advancement, while at home her art and crochet projects piled up half-finished as she jumped from one ambitious idea to another without follow-through. After a tense incident in which she accused a colleague of sabotaging her training and her supervisor documented a marked decline in reliability, she was urged to see her primary care provider, who referred her for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear that these thinking changes were persisting and affecting her ability to work.\n- Chief Complaint: “My mind just won’t slow down—ideas are racing all the time and I can’t keep my thoughts straight anymore.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): I keep thinking some of the other aides and even my teacher from the night class are teaming up to block me from becoming a supervisor, like they’re changing schedules and paperwork on purpose, and no matter what anyone says I just can’t shake the feeling it’s all aimed at me.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): On and off this past year I’ll hear my name whispered or a man’s voice commenting on what I’m doing when I’m alone in my apartment or walking the halls at work, and when I look around there’s nobody there, but it sounds as clear as if someone was right beside me.\n- S022 (Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior): Sometimes at work or even at home I’ll get so wound up or confused that I start pacing the halls, talking to myself under my breath or laughing at things in my head, and then other times I’ll just freeze up in the break room, staring at the wall and not responding when people talk to me because I feel kind of stuck inside myself.\n- S023 (Negative_Symptoms): Compared to how I used to be, it’s like the life has gone out of me a lot of days—I sit on the couch for hours, don’t return calls, my voice feels flat, and even my painting and crochet just sit in piles because I can’t get myself to care enough to pick them up or finish anything.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): I go through stretches where I’m either way up—chatty, overexcited, snapping at people if they slow me down—or I flip and get real edgy and touchy over little things, like a simple question from my supervisor feels like an attack and I find myself raising my voice before I even realize it.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): When I get into those high-energy phases, I’m buzzing around the apartment cleaning, reorganizing my supplies, starting new art projects, signing up for extra shifts and courses, all at the same time, and I just can’t sit still even when my body is tired.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): During those times I honestly feel like I’m meant to fix everything at the facility—like I’ve got this special way with the residents and could run the whole floor better than the nurses if they’d just listen to me, and it doesn’t occur to me that I might be overdoing it until much later.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): For long stretches I’ve been sleeping maybe three or four hours a night, lying there planning new programs for the residents or rearranging my art ideas in my head, and I get up feeling wired instead of sleepy, like I don’t really need rest anymore.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): These days my thoughts are going so fast that while I’m giving someone a bath I’m also thinking about new art groups, then worrying about people talking behind my back, then jumping to how I could start a whole new training program, all piling on each other so I lose track of what I’m actually doing in the moment.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I’m like this I can’t stick with anything—if I’m charting on a resident and hear a TV in the hallway or see someone walk by, my mind shoots over there, then I remember a new idea for a project, then I’m checking my phone, and the original task just sits half-done until someone reminds me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently: When I first started cramming in extra shifts and classes and barely sleeping, feeling on top of the world and snapping at people, that was also when the whispers started and I became convinced my coworkers and teacher were plotting to block my promotion.\n- manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness: Looking back over this whole stretch, I’ve spent most of the time either revved up—taking on too much, sleeping hardly at all, and feeling like I could run the place—or just coming down from those highs but still more keyed up than my old normal.\n- delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode: There was a long spell this past winter when my energy and mood had mostly dropped back to ‘so-so,’ but I was still sure people at work were secretly changing things to sabotage me and I kept hearing my name whispered when I was alone.\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, my charting has gotten so unreliable that my supervisor cut my hours and put me on closer supervision, I’ve fallen behind in my evening course, and I’ve pulled away from family and friends, spending most of my off-time alone on the couch instead of doing the hobbies and visits I used to enjoy.\n- duration: All of this really started after I threw myself into the CNA supervisor push about a year and a half ago, and it’s been going on in one form or another ever since.\n- psychotic_symptoms_duration: The voices and the strong feeling that people are working against me have been coming and going for most of the past year, not just during my most revved-up times.\n- manic_episode_duration: These high-energy, little-sleep, overconfident phases have been a big part of my life for almost the whole time since I started chasing the supervisor role, easing up only briefly before flaring again.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, After a tense incident in which she accused a colleague of sabotaging her training and her supervisor documented a marked decline in reliability, she was urged to see her primary care provider, who referred her for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear that these thinking changes were persisting and affecting her ability to work.\n- Chief Complaint: “My mind just won’t slow down—ideas are racing all the time and I can’t keep my thoughts straight anymore.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): I keep thinking some of the other aides and even my teacher from the night class are teaming up to block me from becoming a supervisor, like they’re changing schedules and paperwork on purpose, and no matter what anyone says I just can’t shake the feeling it’s all aimed at me.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): On and off this past year I’ll hear my name whispered or a man’s voice commenting on what I’m doing when I’m alone in my apartment or walking the halls at work, and when I look around there’s nobody there, but it sounds as clear as if someone was right beside me.\n- S022 (Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior): Sometimes at work or even at home I’ll get so wound up or confused that I start pacing the halls, talking to myself under my breath or laughing at things in my head, and then other times I’ll just freeze up in the break room, staring at the wall and not responding when people talk to me because I feel kind of stuck inside myself.\n- S023 (Negative_Symptoms): Compared to how I used to be, it’s like the life has gone out of me a lot of days—I sit on the couch for hours, don’t return calls, my voice feels flat, and even my painting and crochet just sit in piles because I can’t get myself to care enough to pick them up or finish anything.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): I go through stretches where I’m either way up—chatty, overexcited, snapping at people if they slow me down—or I flip and get real edgy and touchy over little things, like a simple question from my supervisor feels like an attack and I find myself raising my voice before I even realize it.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): When I get into those high-energy phases, I’m buzzing around the apartment cleaning, reorganizing my supplies, starting new art projects, signing up for extra shifts and courses, all at the same time, and I just can’t sit still even when my body is tired.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): During those times I honestly feel like I’m meant to fix everything at the facility—like I’ve got this special way with the residents and could run the whole floor better than the nurses if they’d just listen to me, and it doesn’t occur to me that I might be overdoing it until much later.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): For long stretches I’ve been sleeping maybe three or four hours a night, lying there planning new programs for the residents or rearranging my art ideas in my head, and I get up feeling wired instead of sleepy, like I don’t really need rest anymore.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): These days my thoughts are going so fast that while I’m giving someone a bath I’m also thinking about new art groups, then worrying about people talking behind my back, then jumping to how I could start a whole new training program, all piling on each other so I lose track of what I’m actually doing in the moment.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I’m like this I can’t stick with anything—if I’m charting on a resident and hear a TV in the hallway or see someone walk by, my mind shoots over there, then I remember a new idea for a project, then I’m checking my phone, and the original task just sits half-done until someone reminds me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently: When I first started cramming in extra shifts and classes and barely sleeping, feeling on top of the world and snapping at people, that was also when the whispers started and I became convinced my coworkers and teacher were plotting to block my promotion.\n- manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness: Looking back over this whole stretch, I’ve spent most of the time either revved up—taking on too much, sleeping hardly at all, and feeling like I could run the place—or just coming down from those highs but still more keyed up than my old normal.\n- delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode: There was a long spell this past winter when my energy and mood had mostly dropped back to ‘so-so,’ but I was still sure people at work were secretly changing things to sabotage me and I kept hearing my name whispered when I was alone.\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, my charting has gotten so unreliable that my supervisor cut my hours and put me on closer supervision, I’ve fallen behind in my evening course, and I’ve pulled away from family and friends, spending most of my off-time alone on the couch instead of doing the hobbies and visits I used to enjoy.\n- duration: All of this really started after I threw myself into the CNA supervisor push about a year and a half ago, and it’s been going on in one form or another ever since.\n- psychotic_symptoms_duration: The voices and the strong feeling that people are working against me have been coming and going for most of the past year, not just during my most revved-up times.\n- manic_episode_duration: These high-energy, little-sleep, overconfident phases have been a big part of my life for almost the whole time since I started chasing the supervisor role, easing up only briefly before flaring again.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. The increased demands disrupted her usual balance of solitary painting, gardening, and quiet walks, and she began spending more nights awake planning projects and studying, insisting she could manage on very little rest. Over time, coworkers noticed her documentation became inconsistent and she grew intensely suspicious about others trying to undermine her advancement, while at home her art and crochet projects piled up half-finished as she jumped from one ambitious idea to another without follow-through. After a tense incident in which she accused a colleague of sabotaging her training and her supervisor documented a marked decline in reliability, she was urged to see her primary care provider, who referred her for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear that these thinking changes were persisting and affecting her ability to work.\n- Chief Complaint: “My mind just won’t slow down—ideas are aimed at me.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): On and off this past year I’ll hear my name whispered or a man’s voice commenting on what I’m doing when I’m alone in my apartment or walking the halls at work, and when I look around there’s nobody there, but it sounds as clear as if someone was right beside me.\n- S022 (Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior): Sometimes at work or even at home I’ll get so wound up tired.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): During those times I honestly feel like I’m meant to fix everything at the facility—like I’ve got this special way with the residents and could run the whole floor better than the nurses if they’d just listen to me, and it doesn’t occur to me that I might be overdoing it until much later.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): For long stretches I’ve been sleeping maybe three or four hours a night, lying there planning new programs for the residents or rearranging my art ideas in my head, and I get up feeling wired instead of sleepy, like I don’t really need rest anymore.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): These days my thoughts are going so fast that while I’m giving someone a bath I’m also thinking about new art groups, then worrying about people talking behind my back, then jumping to how I could start a whole new training program, all piling on each other so I lose track of what I’m actually doing in the moment.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I’m like this I can’t stick with anything—if I’m charting on a resident and hear a TV in the hallway or see someone walk by, my mind shoots over there, then I remember a new idea for a project, then I’m stretch, I’ve spent most of the time either revved up—taking on too much, sleeping hardly at all, and feeling like I could run the place—or just coming down from those highs but still more keyed up than my old normal.\n- delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode: There was a long spell this past winter when my energy and mood had mostly dropped back to ‘so-so,’ but I was still sure people at work were secretly changing things to sabotage me and I kept hearing my name whispered when I was alone.\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, my charting has gotten so unreliable that my supervisor cut my hours and put me on closer supervision, I’ve fallen behind in my evening course, and I’ve pulled away from family and friends, spending most of my off-time alone on the couch instead of doing the hobbies and visits I used to enjoy.\n- duration: All of this really started after I threw myself into the CNA supervisor push about a year and a half ago, and it’s been going on in one form or another ever since.\n- psychotic_symptoms_duration: The voices and the strong feeling that people are working against me have been coming and going for most of the past year, not just during my most revved-up times.\n- manic_episode_duration: These high-energy, little-sleep, overconfident phases have been a big part of my life for almost simple question from my supervisor feels like an attack and I find myself raising my voice before I even realize it.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): When I get into those high-energy phases, I’m buzzing around the apartment cleaning, reorganizing my supplies, starting new art projects, signing up for extra shifts and courses, all at the same time, and I just can’t sit still even when my body is maybe three or four hours a night, lying there planning new programs for the residents or rearranging my art ideas in my head, and I get up feeling wired instead of sleepy, like I don’t really need rest anymore.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): These days my thoughts are going so fast that while I’m giving someone a bath I’m also thinking about new art groups, then worrying about people talking behind my back, then jumping to how I could start a whole new training program, all piling on each other so I lose track of what I’m actually doing in the moment.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I’m like this I can’t stick with anything—if I’m charting on a resident and hear a TV in the hallway or see someone walk by, my mind shoots over there, then I remember a new idea for a project, then I’m stretch, I’ve spent most of the time either revved up—taking on too much, sleeping hardly at all, and feeling like I could run the place—or just coming down from those highs but still more keyed up than my old normal.\n- delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode: There was a long spell this past winter when my energy and mood had mostly dropped back to ‘so-so,’ but I was still sure people at work were secretly changing things to sabotage me and I kept hearing my name whispered when I was alone.\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, The increased demands disrupted her usual balance of solitary painting, gardening, and quiet walks, and she began spending more nights awake planning projects and studying, insisting she could manage on very little rest. Over time, coworkers noticed her documentation became inconsistent and she grew intensely suspicious about others trying to undermine her advancement, while at home her art and crochet projects piled up half-finished as she jumped from one ambitious idea to another without follow-through. After a tense incident in which she accused a colleague of sabotaging her training and her supervisor documented a marked decline in reliability, she was urged to see her primary care provider, who referred her for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear that these thinking changes were persisting and affecting her ability to work.\n- Chief Complaint: “My mind just won’t slow down—ideas are racing all the time and I can’t keep my thoughts straight anymore.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S019 (Delusions): I keep thinking some of the other aides and even my teacher from the night class are teaming up to block me from becoming a supervisor, like they’re changing schedules and paperwork on purpose, and no matter what anyone says I just can’t shake the feeling it’s all aimed at me.\n- S020 (Hallucinations): On and off this past year I’ll hear my name whispered or a man’s voice commenting on what I’m doing when I’m alone in my apartment or walking the halls at work, and when I look around there’s nobody there, but it sounds as clear as if someone was right beside me.\n- S022 (Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior): Sometimes at work or even at home I’ll get so wound up or confused that I start pacing the halls, talking to myself under my breath or laughing at things in my head, and then other times I’ll just freeze up in the break room, staring at the wall and not responding when people talk to me because I feel kind of stuck inside myself.\n- S023 (Negative_Symptoms): Compared to how I used to be, it’s like the life has gone out of me a lot of days—I sit on the couch for hours, don’t return calls, my voice feels flat, and even my painting and crochet just sit in piles because I can’t get myself to care enough to pick them up or finish anything.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): I go through stretches where I’m either way up—chatty, overexcited, snapping at people if they slow me down—or I flip and get real edgy and touchy over little things, like a simple question from my supervisor feels like an attack and I find myself raising my voice before I even realize it.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): When I get into those high-energy phases, I’m buzzing around the apartment cleaning, reorganizing my supplies, starting new art projects, signing up for extra shifts and courses, all at the same time, and I just can’t sit still even when my body is tired.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): During those times I honestly feel like I’m meant to fix everything at the facility—like I’ve got this special way with the residents and could run the whole floor better than the nurses if they’d just listen to me, and it doesn’t occur to me that I might be overdoing it until much later.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): For long stretches I’ve been sleeping maybe three or four hours a night, lying there planning new programs for the residents or rearranging my art ideas in my head, and I get up feeling wired instead of sleepy, like I don’t really need rest anymore.\n- S038 (Racing_Thoughts): These days my thoughts are going so fast that while I’m giving someone a bath I’m also thinking about new art groups, then worrying about people talking behind my back, then jumping to how I could start a whole new training program, all piling on each other so I lose track of what I’m actually doing in the moment.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): When I’m like this I can’t stick with anything—if I’m charting on a resident and hear a TV in the hallway or see someone walk by, my mind shoots over there, then I remember a new idea for a project, then I’m checking my phone, and the original task just sits half-done until someone reminds me.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently: When I first started cramming in extra shifts and classes and barely sleeping, feeling on top of the world and snapping at people, that was also when the whispers started and I became convinced my coworkers and teacher were plotting to block my promotion.\n- manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness: Looking back over this whole stretch, I’ve spent most of the time either revved up—taking on too much, sleeping hardly at all, and feeling like I could run the place—or just coming down from those highs but still more keyed up than my old normal.\n- delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode: There was a long spell this past winter when my energy and mood had mostly dropped back to ‘so-so,’ but I was still sure people at work were secretly changing things to sabotage me and I kept hearing my name whispered when I was alone.\n- functional_impairment: Because of all this, my charting has gotten so unreliable that my supervisor cut my hours and put me on closer supervision, I’ve fallen behind in my evening course, and I’ve pulled away from family and friends, spending most of my off-time alone on the couch instead of doing the hobbies and visits I used to enjoy.\n- duration: All of this really started after I threw myself into the CNA supervisor push about a year and a half ago, and it’s been going on in one form or another ever since.\n- psychotic_symptoms_duration: The voices and the strong feeling that people are working against me have been coming and going for most of the past year, not just during my most revved-up times.\n- manic_episode_duration: These high-energy, little-sleep, overconfident phases have been a big part of my life for almost the whole time since I started chasing the supervisor role, easing up only briefly before flaring again.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “My mind just won’t slow down—ideas are racing all the time and I can’t keep my thoughts straight anymore.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. It's just gotten so overwhelming, and I feel like I'm constantly falling behind because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you struggled with these racing thoughts constantly since childhood, or do they come and go in distinct phases?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't something from childhood; it actually started about a year and a half ago when I threw myself into trying to become a CNA supervisor. It definitely comes and goes in phases, and it gets much worse when I'm in those high-energy stretches where I'm barely sleeping.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you are barely sleeping, do you feel exhausted and tired, or do you find that you still have plenty of energy and don't feel the need to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don't feel tired at all during those times, even if I only get three or four hours of sleep. I wake up feeling completely wired and full of energy, as if I don't even need any more rest.\n\nDoctor: On the other hand, do you also experience periods where you feel very down, exhausted, or lose interest in the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have periods where it feels like all the life has gone out of me. I'll just sit on the couch for hours and ignore phone calls, and I completely lose interest in my painting and crochet. All my projects just sit there in unfinished piles because I can't bring myself to care enough to touch them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these high-energy phases with very little sleep, how long do they typically last? In fact, these high-energy phases have been a major, ongoing part of my life for almost the entire year and a half since I started pushing for the supervisor role. They only seem to ease up for a very brief time before flaring right back up again.\n\nDoctor: When you experience those low periods where you lose interest in your hobbies and sit on the couch, how long do they typically last at a time?"

It's just gotten so overwhelming, and I feel like I'm constantly falling behind because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you struggled with these racing thoughts constantly since childhood, or do they come and go in distinct phases?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't something from childhood; it actually started about a year and a half ago when I threw myself into trying to become a CNA supervisor. It definitely comes and goes in phases, and it gets much worse when I'm in those high-energy stretches where I'm barely sleeping.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you are barely sleeping, do you feel exhausted and tired, or do you find that you still have plenty of energy and don't feel the need to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don't feel tired at all during those times, even if I only get three or four hours of sleep. I wake up feeling completely wired and full of energy, as if I don't even need any more rest.\n\nDoctor: On the other hand, do you also experience periods where you feel very down, exhausted, or lose interest in the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have periods where it feels like all the life has gone out of me. I'll just sit on the couch for hours and ignore phone calls, and I completely lose interest in my painting and crochet. All my projects just sit there in unfinished piles because I can't bring myself to care enough to touch them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these high-energy phases with very little sleep, how long do they typically last? Do they go on for several days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: They last much longer than just a few days or a week. In fact, these high-energy phases have been a major, ongoing part of my life for almost the entire year and a half since I started pushing for the supervisor role. They only seem to ease up for a very brief time before flaring right back up again.\n\nDoctor: When you experience those low periods where you lose interest in your hobbies and sit on the couch, how long do they typically last at a time?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure exactly how long those low periods last. I haven't really tracked the exact number of days or weeks, because I tend to completely lose track of time when I'm feeling that down.\n\nDoctor: During those low periods when you feel like all the life has gone out of you, do you also experience significant changes in your appetite, feelings of worthlessness, or thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific things—my appetite is mostly normal, and I don't have thoughts of self-harm or feeling worthless. It's more just that I feel completely flat and empty inside, like I just don't have the energy or care to do anything at all.\n\nDoctor: During your high-energy phases, have you ever made impulsive decisions or taken risks that caused serious problems in your life, such as reckless spending, risky behavior, or major conflicts with others?"
  }

I haven't really tracked the exact number of days or weeks, because I tend to completely lose track of time when I'm feeling that down.\n\nDoctor: During those low periods when you feel like all the life has gone out of you, do you also experience significant changes in your appetite, feelings of worthlessness, or thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific things—my appetite is mostly normal, and I don't have thoughts of self-harm or feeling worthless. It's more just that I feel completely flat and empty inside, like I just don't have the energy or care to do anything at all.\n\nDoctor: During your high-energy phases, have you ever made impulsive decisions or taken risks that caused serious problems in your life, such as reckless spending, risky behavior, or major conflicts with others?\n\nPatient: I don't really do things like reckless spending, but I definitely take on way too much at once by signing up for too many extra shifts and classes. I've also had major conflicts, like snapping at people, raising my voice at my supervisor, and accusing a colleague of sabotaging my training. That tense incident actually led to my hours being cut and me being referred here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and a half, did you ever experience milder ups and downs in your mood, or was your mood completely stable throughout your life before then?"

It's more just that I feel completely flat and empty inside, like I just don't have the energy or care to do anything at all.\n\nDoctor: During your high-energy phases, have you ever made impulsive decisions or taken risks that caused serious problems in your life, such as reckless spending, risky behavior, or major conflicts with others?\n\nPatient: I don't really do things like reckless spending, but I definitely take on way too much at once by signing up for too many extra shifts and classes. I've also had major conflicts, like snapping at people, raising my voice at my supervisor, and accusing a colleague of sabotaging my training. That tense incident actually led to my hours being cut and me being referred here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and a half, did you ever experience milder ups and downs in your mood, or was your mood completely stable throughout your life before then?\n\nPatient: Before a year and a half ago, I felt pretty stable and balanced with my usual routine of painting, gardening, and taking quiet walks. I don't really remember having any notable ups and downs like this back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these low, empty periods on multiple different occasions over the past year and a half, or has it only happened once?"

It's just gotten so overwhelming, and I feel like I'm constantly falling behind because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you struggled with these racing thoughts constantly since childhood, or do they come and go in distinct phases?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't something from childhood; it actually started about a year and a half ago when I threw myself into trying to become a CNA supervisor. It definitely comes and goes in phases, and it gets much worse when I'm in those high-energy stretches where I'm barely sleeping.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you are barely sleeping, do you feel exhausted and tired, or do you find that you still have plenty of energy and don't feel the need to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don't feel tired at all during those times, even if I only get three or four hours of sleep. I wake up feeling completely wired and full of energy, as if I don't even need any more rest.\n\nDoctor: On the other hand, do you also experience periods where you feel very down, exhausted, or lose interest in the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have periods where it feels like all the life has gone out of me. I'll just sit on the couch for hours and ignore phone calls, and I completely lose interest in my painting and crochet. All my projects just sit there in unfinished piles because I can't bring myself to care enough to touch them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these high-energy phases with very little sleep, how long do they typically last? Do they go on for several days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: They last much longer than just a few days or a week. In fact, these high-energy phases have been a major, ongoing part of my life for almost the entire year and a half since I started pushing for the supervisor role. They only seem to ease up for a very brief time before flaring right back up again.\n\nDoctor: When you experience those low periods where you lose interest in your hobbies and sit on the couch, how long do they typically last at a time?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure exactly how long those low periods last. I haven't really tracked the exact number of days or weeks, because I tend to completely lose track of time when I'm feeling that down.\n\nDoctor: During those low periods when you feel like all the life has gone out of you, do you also experience significant changes in your appetite, feelings of worthlessness, or thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific things—my appetite is mostly normal, and I don't have thoughts of self-harm or feeling worthless. It's more just that I feel completely flat and empty inside, like I just don't have the energy or care to do anything at all.\n\nDoctor: During your high-energy phases, have you ever made impulsive decisions or taken risks that caused serious problems in your life, such as reckless spending, risky behavior, or major conflicts with others?\n\nPatient: I don't really do things like reckless spending, but I definitely take on way too much at once by signing up for too many extra shifts and classes. I've also had major conflicts, like snapping at people, raising my voice at my supervisor, and accusing a colleague of sabotaging my training. That tense incident actually led to my hours being cut and me being referred here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and a half, did you ever experience milder ups and downs in your mood, or was your mood completely stable throughout your life before then?\n\nPatient: Before a year and a half ago, I felt pretty stable and balanced with my usual routine of painting, gardening, and taking quiet walks. I don't really remember having any notable ups and downs like this back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these low, empty periods on multiple different occasions over the past year and a half, or has it only happened once?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about having distinct 'low' episodes that come and go. It feels more like a constant flatness or loss of energy on many days, like the life has just gone out of me, rather than separate, distinct periods of depression. Because of that, I'm really not sure how to count them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience that flatness or loss of energy, does it ever last continuously for at least two weeks at a time, where you feel that way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure of the exact timing of those feelings. It happens on a lot of days, but it's hard to say if it lasts for two weeks straight because everything just seems to blend together.\n\nDoctor: During those flat periods when you lost interest in things, did you find it difficult to perform your job or manage your daily responsibilities at home?"
  }

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: My mind just won’t slow down—ideas are racing all the time and I can’t keep my thoughts straight anymore. It's just gotten so overwhelming, and I feel like I'm constantly falling behind because of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you struggled with these racing thoughts constantly since childhood, or do they come and go in distinct phases?\n\nPatient: No, this isn't something from childhood; it actually started about a year and a half ago when I threw myself into trying to become a CNA supervisor. It definitely comes and goes in phases, and it gets much worse when I'm in those high-energy stretches where I'm barely sleeping.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you are barely sleeping, do you feel exhausted and tired, or do you find that you still have plenty of energy and don't feel the need to sleep?\n\nPatient: I don't feel tired at all during those times, even if I only get three or four hours of sleep. I wake up feeling completely wired and full of energy, as if I don't even need any more rest.\n\nDoctor: On the other hand, do you also experience periods where you feel very down, exhausted, or lose interest in the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have periods where it feels like all the life has gone out of me. I'll just sit on the couch for hours and ignore phone calls, and I completely lose interest in my painting and crochet. All my projects just sit there in unfinished piles because I can't bring myself to care enough to touch them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these high-energy phases with very little sleep, how long do they typically last? Do they go on for several days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: They last much longer than just a few days or a week. In fact, these high-energy phases have been a major, ongoing part of my life for almost the entire year and a half since I started pushing for the supervisor role. They only seem to ease up for a very brief time before flaring right back up again.\n\nDoctor: When you experience those low periods where you lose interest in your hobbies and sit on the couch, how long do they typically last at a time?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure exactly how long those low periods last. I haven't really tracked the exact number of days or weeks, because I tend to completely lose track of time when I'm feeling that down.\n\nDoctor: During those low periods when you feel like all the life has gone out of you, do you also experience significant changes in your appetite, feelings of worthlessness, or thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific things—my appetite is mostly normal, and I don't have thoughts of self-harm or feeling worthless. It's more just that I feel completely flat and empty inside, like I just don't have the energy or care to do anything at all.\n\nDoctor: During your high-energy phases, have you ever made impulsive decisions or taken risks that caused serious problems in your life, such as reckless spending, risky behavior, or major conflicts with others?\n\nPatient: I don't really do things like reckless spending, but I definitely take on way too much at once by signing up for too many extra shifts and classes. I've also had major conflicts, like snapping at people, raising my voice at my supervisor, and accusing a colleague of sabotaging my training. That tense incident actually led to my hours being cut and me being referred here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and a half, did you ever experience milder ups and downs in your mood, or was your mood completely stable throughout your life before then?\n\nPatient: Before a year and a half ago, I felt pretty stable and balanced with my usual routine of painting, gardening, and taking quiet walks. I don't really remember having any notable ups and downs like this back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these low, empty periods on multiple different occasions over the past year and a half, or has it only happened once?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about having distinct 'low' episodes that come and go. Because of that, I'm really not sure how to count them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience that flatness or loss of energy, does it ever last continuously for at least two weeks at a time, where you feel that way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure of the exact timing of those feelings. It happens on a lot of days, but it's hard to say if it lasts for two weeks straight because everything just seems to blend together.\n\nDoctor: During those flat periods when you lost interest in things, did you find it difficult to perform your job or manage your daily responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it was incredibly hard to get anything done during those flat periods. At home, I would just sit on the couch for hours, ignore phone calls, and let my art and crochet projects pile up. My performance at work and school also slipped significantly—my charting became so unreliable that my supervisor cut my hours, and I fell behind in my evening classes.\n\nDoctor: During the past year and a half, have you had any periods lasting at least a few weeks where your mood felt completely normal and you felt like your usual self, in between the high-energy and flat phases?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any times where I felt completely normal or like my old self over the last year and a half. I wake up feeling completely wired and full of energy, as if I don't even need any more rest.\n\nDoctor: On the other hand, do you also experience periods where you feel very down, exhausted, or lose interest in the things you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do have periods where it feels like all the life has gone out of me. I'll just sit on the couch for hours and ignore phone calls, and I completely lose interest in my painting and crochet. All my projects just sit there in unfinished piles because I can't bring myself to care enough to touch them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these high-energy phases with very little sleep, how long do they typically last? Do they go on for several days, a week, or even longer?\n\nPatient: They last much longer than just a few days or a week. In fact, these high-energy phases have been a major, ongoing part of my life for almost the entire year and a half since I started pushing for the supervisor role. They only seem to ease up for a very brief time before flaring right back up again.\n\nDoctor: When you experience those low periods where you lose interest in your hobbies and sit on the couch, how long do they typically last at a time?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure exactly how long those low periods last. I haven't really tracked the exact number of days or weeks, because I tend to completely lose track of time when I'm feeling that down.\n\nDoctor: During those low periods when you feel like all the life has gone out of you, do you also experience significant changes in your appetite, feelings of worthlessness, or thoughts of self-harm?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those specific things—my appetite is mostly normal, and I don't have thoughts of self-harm or feeling worthless. It's more just that I feel completely flat and empty inside, like I just don't have the energy or care to do anything at all.\n\nDoctor: During your high-energy phases, have you ever made impulsive decisions or taken risks that caused serious problems in your life, such as reckless spending, risky behavior, or major conflicts with others?\n\nPatient: I don't really do things like reckless spending, but I definitely take on way too much at once by signing up for too many extra shifts and classes. I've also had major conflicts, like snapping at people, raising my voice at my supervisor, and accusing a colleague of sabotaging my training. That tense incident actually led to my hours being cut and me being referred here for this evaluation.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and a half, did you ever experience milder ups and downs in your mood, or was your mood completely stable throughout your life before then?\n\nPatient: Before a year and a half ago, I felt pretty stable and balanced with my usual routine of painting, gardening, and taking quiet walks. I don't really remember having any notable ups and downs like this back then.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these low, empty periods on multiple different occasions over the past year and a half, or has it only happened once?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure about having distinct 'low' episodes that come and go. It feels more like a constant flatness or loss of energy on many days, like the life has just gone out of me, rather than separate, distinct periods of depression. Because of that, I'm really not sure how to count them.\n\nDoctor: When you experience that flatness or loss of energy, does it ever last continuously for at least two weeks at a time, where you feel that way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure of the exact timing of those feelings. It happens on a lot of days, but it's hard to say if it lasts for two weeks straight because everything just seems to blend together.\n\nDoctor: During those flat periods when you lost interest in things, did you find it difficult to perform your job or manage your daily responsibilities at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, it was incredibly hard to get anything done during those flat periods. At home, I would just sit on the couch for hours, ignore phone calls, and let my art and crochet projects pile up. My performance at work and school also slipped significantly—my charting became so unreliable that my supervisor cut my hours, and I fell behind in my evening classes.\n\nDoctor: During the past year and a half, have you had any periods lasting at least a few weeks where your mood felt completely normal and you felt like your usual self, in between the high-energy and flat phases?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really had any times where I felt completely normal or like my old self over the last year and a half. Even when the high-energy phases ease up briefly, I still feel keyed up, or my mood drops to just 'so-so' while I continue to hear those whispered voices and feel like people are trying to sabotage me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced hearing those whispered voices or feeling like others are trying to sabotage you during a time when your mood felt relatively normal and stable for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes, that actually happened this past winter during a long stretch where my mood and energy had leveled off and felt relatively normal, or just 'so-so'. Even though my mood wasn't super high or low, I was still hearing those whispered voices when I was alone and felt convinced that people at work were secretly changing things to sabotage me.\n\nDoctor: During that period last winter when your mood was relatively stable, did those whispered voices and feelings of sabotage last for at least two weeks continuously?\n\nPatient: Yes, those voices and the feeling of being sabotaged definitely lasted continuously for well over two weeks during that time. Even though my mood and energy had leveled off to just \"so-so,\" those experiences kept happening day after day without any break."

Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
